### TO
## The Reader.

*Judicious Reader,*

<margin>* Dr. Everard.</margin>
This book may rightly claim the first place for antiquity among all the *books* in the *world*, being written some hundreds of years before the time of *Moses*, as I shall endeavor to prove <note>original: "make good"</note>. The *original* (as far as is known to us) is *Arabic*, and several translations of it have been published, such as *Greek*, *Latin*, *French*, *Dutch*, *etc.*, but never in *English* before. It is a pity the *learned* *translator* <note>John Everard (c. 1584–1640) was a prominent preacher and translator known for his interest in mystical and Hermetic texts.</note> did not live to receive for himself the honor and thanks due to him from the *English*; for his goodwill and efforts for

<summary>The preface introduces this work as a translation of an ancient text allegedly predating Moses, noting that while it has appeared in other languages, this is its first English edition. It also commemorates the late translator, Dr. John Everard, for his efforts in bringing the text to an English-speaking audience.</summary>

<keywords>Dr. John Everard, antiquity, translation, Arabic, Moses, Hermetica, English translation</keywords>

Concerning the author of the book itself, four things are worthy of consideration <note>original: "considerable"</note>: namely, his name, learning, country, and time.

1. The name by which he was commonly called <note>original: "styled"</note> is *Hermes Trismegistus*, which means *Mercury the thrice greatest* <note>original: "Mercurius ter Maximus"</note>, or the thrice greatest messenger of knowledge <note>original: "Intelligencer"</note>. And he might well be called *Hermes*, for he was the first messenger in the world (as we read) who communicated knowledge to the sons of men by writing or engraving. He was called *thrice greatest* <note>original: "Ter Maximus"</note> for several reasons, which I shall mention later. 

2. His learning will be shown both by his works and by correctly understanding the meaning behind his name. 

3. As for his country—

But if this is their only evidence <note>original: "ground"</note>, you must excuse my disagreement with them. Therefore, the reason previously alleged to prove that this author lived after *Moses* seems invalid. Nor does it appear in any way that he lived during *Moses's* time, although it is the opinion of some—such as *Johann Funck* <note>original: "John Functius" (1518–1566), a German chronologist and theologian</note>, who states in his *Chronology* that Hermes lived twenty-one years before the Law was given by *Moses* in the *wilderness*.

## To the Reader.

...that he shall become more and more religious.

The glory and splendor of philosophy is the effort to understand the highest Good as the Source <note>original: "Fountain"</note> of all good. Now, how can we come near to or find the Source except by using the streams as a guide <note>original: "conduct"</note> to it? The operations of nature are streams flowing from the Source of Good, which is God. I do not hold the ignorant and foolish opinion of those who say that the greatest philosophers are the greatest atheists—as if knowing the works of God and understanding His ways in the natural world <note>original: "Way of Nature"</note> must force a man to deny God.

# Hermes Trismegistus

## HIS First Book

To my son, I write this first book, both for humanity's sake and for piety <note>original: "Piety"; meaning religious devotion and duty.</note> toward God.

2. For there can be no religion more true or just than to know the things that exist <note>original: "the things that are"; referring to the true nature of reality.</note>, and to give thanks for all things to Him who made them—something I shall never stop doing.

3. What then should a person do, O Father, to live well, since there is nothing here that is true <note>original: "true"; in this context, "true" refers to the eternal and unchanging reality, contrasting with the illusory material world.</note>?

4. Be pious and religious, O my son; for he who does so is the best and highest philosopher. And without philosophy,

# *Hermes Trismegistus*

## **HIS**
## *First Book.*

To my son: write this first book, both for humanity’s sake and out of piety <note>original: "Piety" (devotion or religious reverence)</note> toward God.

2. For there can be no religion more true or just than to know the things that exist <note>original: "the things that are"</note> and to give thanks for all things to Him who made them. This is something I will never stop doing.

3. What then should a man do, O Father, to lead his life well, seeing that nothing here is true?

4. Be pious and religious, O my son; for he who does so is the best and highest philosopher. And without philosophy,

And he who gives thanks shall be pious or religious; and he who is religious shall know both where the truth is and what it is. By learning that, he will become yet more and more religious.

10. But the victory of both is not the same; for the one hurries toward that which is **Good**, but the other is a neighbor to things that are **Evil**. That which is **Good** desires to be set at **Liberty**; but the things that are **Evil** love **Bondage** and **Slavery**.

11. <meta>This page marks the beginning of the second book of the collection. While the previous book concluded with a warning about keeping divine secrets from the unworthy, this book opens with Hermes Trismegistus entering a deep, meditative state where he receives a direct revelation from a divine entity.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>*Hermes Trismegistus*</header>

### THE
# Second Book
### CALLED
## *Poemander.* <note>Also known as *Pymander*, from the Greek *Poimandres*, meaning "Shepherd of Men." It represents the Divine Mind or the intelligence of God.</note>

My thoughts being once seriously occupied <note>original: "busied"</note> with the things that exist, and my understanding being lifted up, all my physical senses were exceedingly held back—just as it is with those who are in a very deep sleep, caused either by being full of food <note>original: "meat"</note> or by physical labor. I thought I saw someone of an extraordinarily tall stature and infinite greatness call me by my name and say to me: **"What would you hear and see? Or what would you like to understand, to learn, and to know?"**

<meta>catchword: 2. Then</meta>

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus enters a deep trance-like state where his physical senses are suspended, allowing him to encounter a divine being of infinite stature. This being, Poemander, asks Hermes what he wishes to learn and understand about the nature of reality.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Poemander, vision, Divine Mind, meditation, revelation, spiritual awakening</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and the divine being. After the initial encounter on the previous page, the entity identifies itself and offers to reveal the secrets of the universe to Hermes.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>The Second Book of</header>

2. Then I said, **"Who are you?"** "I am," he said, "**Poemander** <note>original: "Poeman-der"; likely derived from the Greek *Poimandres*, meaning "Shepherd of Men," representing the Divine Mind.</note>, the mind of the great **Lord**, the most mighty and absolute **Emperor**. I know what you desire <note>original: "wouldst have"</note>, and I am always present with you."

3. Then I said, **"I would like to learn about the things that exist, to understand their nature, and to know God."** "How?" he asked. I answered that I would gladly hear it. Then he said, "Keep me in your mind, and whatever you would like to learn, I will teach you."

4. When he had said this, his **Idea** <note>original: "Idea"; in Hermetic and Platonic philosophy, this refers to the eternal, perfect archetype or spiritual essence of a thing.</note> or **Form** changed, and immediately, in the twinkling of an eye, all things were opened to me. And I saw an infinite sight: all things had become light, both sweet and exceedingly pleasant, and I was wonderfully delighted to behold it.

<meta>catchword: 5. But</meta>

<summary>Hermes identifies the divine being as Poemander, the Mind of the Lord, and asks to understand the nature of reality and God. In response, Poemander transforms and grants Hermes a mystical vision of the universe as a sweet and infinite light.</summary>
<keywords>Poemander, Divine Mind, Hermes Trismegistus, revelation, spiritual vision, Idea, Form, nature of reality</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the visionary experience of Hermes Trismegistus. On the previous page, Poemander (the Divine Mind) revealed a vision of infinite light. Here, the vision shifts as darkness and the elements begin to emerge and organize.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus</header>

5. But after a little while, a darkness appeared in part, descending obliquely—fearful and hideous—which seemed to me to change into a certain **moist nature** <note>original: "moyſt nature"; in Hermetic cosmogony, this refers to the primordial chaos or the watery substance from which the material world is formed.</note>, unspeakably troubled, which yielded smoke as if from a fire. From there, an unutterable and very mournful voice proceeded, yet it was inarticulate, sounding as though it had come from the Light.

6. Then from that Light, a certain holy **Word** <note>original: "Word"; representing the Greek *Logos*, the divine reason or creative speech that brings order to chaos.</note> joined itself to **Nature**, and the pure and unmixed **Fire** flew out from the moist **Nature** upward toward the heights. It was exceedingly light, sharp, and active. And the **Air**, being so light, followed the **Spirit** <note>original: "Spirit"; here referring to a refined, airy substance or the breath of life.</note> and rose up to the **Fire** (leaving the **Earth** and the **Water**) so that it seemed to hang and depend upon it.

7. And

<summary>Hermes witnesses the emergence of a "moist nature" (chaos) from darkness and the subsequent separation of the elements—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—triggered by the arrival of the holy Word.</summary>
<keywords>moist nature, chaos, Holy Word, Logos, elements, Fire, Air, Spirit, cosmogony, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the visionary dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Pymander (the Divine Mind). On the previous page, the elements began to separate from the primordial chaos, with Fire and Air rising above Earth and Water.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>The Second Book of Hermes Trismegistus</header>

7. And the Earth and the Water remained together, so thoroughly mixed that the Earth could not be seen through the Water; but they were moved because of the **Spiritual Word** <note>original: "Spirituall Word"; the *Logos*, or the divine creative expression that brings order to chaos.</note> that was carried upon them.

8. Then **Pymander** <note>original: "Poemander"; also known as Poimandres, the "Shepherd of Men" or the personification of the Divine Mind.</note> said to me, "Do you understand this **Vision**, and what it means?"

"I shall soon know," I said.

Then he said, "**I am that Light—the Mind—your God,** who existed before that **moist Nature** <note>original: "moyst Nature"; the primordial, watery substance of the physical world before it was shaped by divine will.</note> appeared out of the darkness; and that **bright and radiant Word** from the **Mind is the Son of God.**"

9. "How is that?" I asked.

"Understand it this way," he replied: "**That which in you sees and hears the Word of the Lord, and the Mind—the Father and God—are not separate from each other; and the union of these is Life.**"

<summary>Pymander explains the vision to Hermes, identifying himself as the Divine Mind and Light, while revealing that the "Word" is the Son of God and that the union of Mind and Word within a person constitutes Life.</summary>
<keywords>Pymander, Divine Mind, Logos, Word of God, moist nature, Hermes Trismegistus, light, creation myth</keywords>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

**Trismegistus.** I thank you. 

**Pimander.** But first, visualize the Light clearly in your mind, and understand it.

10. When he had said this, we looked steadily at one another for a long time; I trembled so much at his **Idea** <note>original: "Idea"; in this context, the word refers to the divine, intellectual pattern or essence of a being rather than just a "thought."</note> or **Form**.

11. But when he nodded to me, I saw in my mind the Light that consists of countless powers, and the truly infinite **ornament** <note>original: "ornament"; used here in the sense of the Greek *kosmos*, meaning a beautifully ordered world or universe.</note> or **world**. I saw that the fire is contained by a most great Power and forced to keep its station.

12. I understood these things by perceiving the word of **Pimander** <note>original: "Pimander"; also known as Poimandres, the personification of the Divine Mind.</note>. While I was struck with great amazement, he spoke to me again: "Have you seen in your mind that Archetypal Form <note>original: "Archetypall Form"; the original blueprint or divine model upon which all of creation is based.</note>, which existed before the endless and infinite Beginning?"

<summary>Hermes experiences a profound vision of the infinite Light and the "ornament" of the world, while Pymander asks if he recognizes the Archetypal Form that existed before the beginning of time.</summary>

### THE

# *The Key.*

Yesterday’s speech, O Asclepius, I dedicated to you; today’s speech is fitting to dedicate to Tat, because it is a summary <note>original: "Epitome"</note> of those general speeches that were spoken to him.

2. God, therefore, and the Father, and the Good, O Tat, have the same nature, or rather, they have the same activity and operation <note>original: "Act and Operation"; in Hermetic philosophy, God's "act" is his very existence and the act of creation</note>.

3. For there is one name or title <note>original: "appellation"</note> of nature and growth <note>original: "Increase"</note> which concerns changeable things, and another concerning things unchangeable, and about things...

unchangeable things <note>original: "unmoveable"</note>, that is to say, things divine and human; every one of which, he himself wills to be so. But action or operation <note>original: "Act or Operation"</note> belongs to something else, or originates elsewhere, as we have taught concerning other things, both divine and human, which must also be understood here.

4. **For**, his **operation** or **act**, is his **will**, and his **essence**—that is, to will all things into existence.

5. For what is God, the Father, and the Good, but the very Being of all things that do not yet exist, and the existence itself of those things that do exist?

6. This is God, this is the Father, and this is the Good, to which no other thing is equal or even approaches.

7. # The Fourth Book of [Hermes Trismegistus]

...and the Good, because He is all things; for He both wills to be this, and actually is it. And yet, all this is for Himself (as is true), as understood by those who are able to see it.

11. For everything else exists for this purpose: it is the very nature <note>original: "property"</note> of the Good to be known. This is the Good, O **Tat**.

12. **Tat**: You have filled us, O **Father**, with a vision <note>original: "sight"</note> that is both good and beautiful; the eye of my mind has almost become more holy by witnessing this spectacle.

13. **Trismegistus**: I am not surprised at this, for the vision of **God** is not like a ray of the **Sun**. The Sun, because of its fiery and shining brightness, blinds the eye that gazes upon it with its excessive light. Rather, the opposite is true: the divine vision enlightens the eye and increases its internal light to the extent that a person is able to receive its spiritual influence <note>original: "influence"; here referring to the flowing in of divine power or grace</note>.

23. **Trism.** Have you not heard in the general Discourses <note>original: "Speeches"</note> that from one Soul of the universe come all those souls which are tossed up and down throughout the whole world, as if they were individually divided? Among these souls there are many changes: some into a more fortunate state, and some quite the opposite. For the souls of creeping things are changed into those of water creatures; and those of things living in the water are changed into those of things living upon the land; and those of the air <note>original: "Airy ones"</note> are changed into men. Human souls that lay hold of immortality are changed into **Demons.** <note>original: "Demons"; in this context, the term refers to "daemons," which are guardian spirits or semi-divine beings that act as intermediaries between gods and humans, rather than evil spirits.</note>

28. On the contrary, the virtue of the soul is knowledge; for he who knows is both good and religious, and is already divine.

29. **Tat.** But who is such a person, O Father?

30. **Trismegistus.** He who neither speaks nor hears many things; for he, my son, who listens to many opinions or sounds fights in the shadow <note>original: "fighteth in the shadow"; likely referring to the confusion caused by the material world or conflicting ideas compared to the singular truth of God</note>.

31. For God, and the Father, and the Good, can neither be spoken of nor heard.

32. This being so, the **senses** exist in all things that are, because things cannot exist without them.

33. But knowledge differs greatly from sense; for sense deals with things that surpass it <note>original: "surmount"; referring to physical objects or external forces that act upon the sensory organs</note>, but knowledge is the goal <note>original: "the end"; meaning the ultimate purpose or completion</note> of sense.

34. Knowledge is the gift of God; for all knowledge is incorporeal <note>original: "unbodily"</note>, but it uses the mind as

<sig>D 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: an</meta>

<summary>Hermes explains that the soul's virtue is found in knowledge and divine focus rather than the distractions of the physical world. He distinguishes between the physical senses, which are present in all things, and divine knowledge, which is a gift from God that transcends physical sensation.</summary>

35. And it is impossible for it to be otherwise.

36. **Tat.** Who, then, is this material God?

37. **Trismegistus.** The fair and beautiful world, and yet it is not "Good"; for it is material and easily affected <note>original: "passible"; meaning capable of being acted upon, changed, or suffering from external influences</note>. Indeed, it is the first of all things that can be affected; it is the second of all things that exist, and it is needy, or lacking something else. It was once created, yet it exists always; it is forever in the process of becoming <note>original: "generation"</note> and being made, and it continually creates, or generates, things that have quantity and quality.

38. For it is movable, and

<meta>catchword: every</meta>

<summary>Trismegistus explains that the material world is a "material God" which, while beautiful, is not inherently "Good" because it is subject to change and depends on higher forces. He describes the world as an ongoing process of creation and transformation driven by the tension between opposing forces.</summary>

Every material motion is generation <note>original: "generation"; the process of coming into being, birth, or creation</note>; but intellectual stability moves material motion in this way:

39. Because the World is a sphere—that is, a head—and above the head there is nothing material, just as beneath the feet there is nothing intellectual.

40. The whole universe is material: the Mind is the head, and it is moved spherically—that is, in the circular manner of a head.

41. Whatever is joined or united to the membrane or film of this head, where the Soul is located, is immortal. Anything that is in the Soul of a created body has its Soul filled with the Body; but those things that are further from that membrane have the Body filled with Soul <note>The author suggests a hierarchy where being close to the "head" or divine mind means the soul dominates the body, whereas being further away means the physical body dominates the soul.</note>.

43. 44. For the World is not good, insofar as it is movable; nor is it evil, insofar as it is immortal.

45. But Man is evil, both because he is movable, and because he is mortal.

46. But the Soul of Man is carried in this manner: **The Mind is in Reason, Reason is in the Soul, the Soul is in the Spirit, and the Spirit is in the Body.**

This is the death of the Body.

49. All things depend on one beginning, and that beginning depends on the One who is alone.

50. And the beginning is moved so that it may be a beginning once again; but that which is One stands still, remains, and is not moved.

## The Communion of Gods with Men, and of Men with Gods.

83. 85. **Trismegistus**: Know, Son, that every soul has the **Divine Mind**; for that is what we are speaking of now, and not of that **ministering spirit** <note>original: "Minister"</note> whom we mentioned before, who was sent from **Judgment**.

86. For the soul without the Mind can neither do nor say anything. Often the Mind departs from the soul, and in that moment, the soul neither sees nor hears but is like a creature without reason <note>original: "unreasonable thing"</note>; so great is the power of the Mind.

92. And what is the greatest thing of all: he does not leave the Earth, and yet he is above it. Such is the greatness of his nature. <note>This refers to the human ability to remain physically on earth while the mind or soul ascends to the divine.</note>

93. Therefore, we must be bold enough to say **that an earthly man is a mortal God, and that the heavenly God is an immortal man.**

94. Consequently, all things are governed by these two: the World and Man; but both of them, and all other things, proceed from that which is **One.** <note>The "One" refers to the Monad or the ultimate Source/God from which all existence originates.</note>

->*The End of the Fourth Book*<-

# THE Fifth Book.

## *That God is not manifest, and yet most manifest.*

1. I will also share this discourse with you, O Tat, <note>Tat is the son and disciple of Hermes Trismegistus in the Hermetic tradition.</note> so that you may not be ignorant of the more excellent Name of God.

2. But contemplate in your mind how that which seems hidden and unmanifest <note>original: "unmanifest"; meaning not clearly visible or obvious to the physical senses.</note> to many may be most manifest to you.

3. For it would not be the "All" if it were apparent; for whatever is apparent is generated <note>original: "generated"; meaning created or brought into being within the physical world.</note> or made, because it was made manifest.

<summary>This page introduces the Fifth Book, where Hermes teaches Tat that while God remains hidden from the physical senses, He is the most apparent reality to the mind, as He is the source of all things.</summary>

# THE SIXTH BOOK

## That in God Alone is Good

1. Good, O Asclepius, exists in nothing except in God alone; or rather, God himself is always the Good.

2. And if this is so, then he must be an Essence or Substance, void of all motion and generation <note>original: "generation"; refers to the process of being born, changing, or coming into existence</note>; yet nothing is void or empty of him.

3. And this Essence has within or around himself a **stable** and firm **operation**, lacking <note>original: "wanting"</note> nothing, completely full, and giving abundantly.

4. One thing is the Beginning of 

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the Sixth Book, establishing the fundamental Hermetic principle that "The Good" is an inherent quality of God's unchanging essence. It describes God as a stable, self-sufficient substance that lacks nothing and provides for all of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetica, Asclepius, The Good, Essence, Substance, Divine Nature, Theology, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the Sixth Book of the Divine Pymander. It develops the argument that "The Good" is a unique property of the Divine because God is self-sufficient and free from the passions and deficiencies that define human existence.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus</header>

...of all things, for it gives all things; and when I name "the Good," I mean that which is altogether and always Good.

5. This is present to no one but God alone; for he lacks <note>original: "wanteth"</note> nothing that he should desire to have, nor can anything be taken from him, the loss of which might grieve him; for sorrow is a part of evil.

6. Nothing is stronger than he is, such that he could be opposed by it; nor is there anything equal to him, such that he should be in love with it; nothing is unknown <note>original: "unheard of"</note> to be angry with, and nothing is wiser to be envious of.

7. And since none of these <term>passions</term> <note>In this context, the author is referring to the "evils" of human emotion like anger, envy, and grief.</note> exist in his Essence, what remains but only the Good?

8. For just as in this Essence there is no evil, so in nothing else shall the Good be found.

<summary>This page argues that true "Goodness" belongs only to God, as he is a self-sufficient being free from the earthly evils of sorrow, anger, and envy. Because God's essence lacks nothing and faces no opposition, it is defined solely by Goodness.</summary>

9. For all things are contained within all other things, whether small or great, and in specific parts just as in this great Living Being <note>original: "living Creature"; in Hermetic philosophy, the Universe itself is viewed as a single, living organism.</note>, which is the greatest and mightiest of all.

10. For everything that is created or <term>generated</term> <note>In this context, "generation" refers to the process of being born or brought into physical existence.</note> is full of <term>passion</term> <note>original: "Passion"; here meaning the state of being acted upon or undergoing change and suffering, rather than modern romantic passion.</note>, as the very process of being born is itself a form of passion. Where passion exists, "the Good" is not found. Where the Good is, there is no passion. Just as where it is day, it is not night, and where it is night, it is not day.

11. Therefore, it is impossible for "the Good" to exist within the process of generation; it is only found in that which is uncreated and unmade.

12. Yet, just as all things are bound to participate in <term>Matter</term>, they also participate in that which is Good. In this way, the World is considered good, insofar as it creates <note>original: "maketh"</note>

all things; and in the act of making or doing (<term>to create</term> <note>original: "ποιεῖν" (poiein); the Greek root for "poetry" or "making."</note>) it is Good, but in all other things, it is not Good.

13. For the world is <term>passible</term> <note>original: "passible"; meaning subject to change, suffering, or being acted upon by external forces, as opposed to being unchangeable.</note> and movable, and it is the Maker of things that are likewise passible.

14. In man also, the Good is defined (or receives its name) only in comparison to that which is evil. For that which is not very evil is considered "Good" here; and that which is called "Good" here is actually the smallest particle or proportion of evil.

15. It is impossible, therefore, for the Good to be pure from Evil here on earth. For here, the Good grows into Evil; and in growing into Evil, it does not remain Good; and by not remaining Good, it becomes evil.

16. Therefore, in God alone is the Good—or rather, God is the Good.

### The Sixth Book on the **Essence** of the Fair and the Good.

24. Just as the eye cannot see God, it likewise cannot see the Fair <note>original: "Fair"; referring to the concept of the Beautiful or Noble (Greek: *to kalon*).</note> or the Good.

25. For these are the parts of God that share in the nature of the whole. They belong specifically and intimately to him alone; they are inseparable and most lovely. God is either enamored with them, or they are enamored with God.

26. If you can understand God, you shall understand the **Fair** and the **Good** <note>The OCR text lists "the God" here, likely an error for "the Good," which completes the philosophical pairing used throughout this section.</note>, which are most radiant, enlightening, and are themselves most enlightened by God.

27. For that Beauty is beyond comparison, and that Good is inimitable <term>Inimitable</term> <note>Meaning something so unique and perfect that it cannot be copied or reproduced.</note>, just like God himself.

28. Therefore, as you understand God, so you must understand the Fair and the Good; for...

<meta>catchword: these</meta>

<summary>Hermes explains that Beauty and Goodness are invisible, divine attributes that are inseparable from God's own nature. He argues that just as God is beyond human sight and comparison, so too are the absolute forms of the Fair and the Good.</summary>

<header>Hermes Trismegistus</header>

...these are incommunicable <term>Incommunicable</term> <note>original: "incommunicable"; in this context, it means these divine qualities cannot be shared with or transferred to any other created beings.</note> to any other living creatures, because they are inseparable from God.

29. If you seek concerning God, you also seek or ask about the **Fair** <note>original: "the Fair"; referring to the Greek concept of *to kalon*, which encompasses both beauty and moral nobility.</note>; for there is one way that leads to the same thing, and that is **Piety** combined with **Knowledge**.

30. Therefore, those who are ignorant and do not walk in the way of *Piety* dare to call *Man* *Fair* and *Good*, never seeing even in a dream what the true *Good* is. Instead, being entangled and wrapped up in all evil, and believing that evil is the *Good*, they use it insatiably. They are afraid to be deprived of it, and therefore they strive by all possible means not only to possess it, but also to increase it.

51. For the number ten, O Son, is the begetter of souls. And there, Life and Light are united where the number of Unity is born of the Spirit.

52. Therefore, according to Reason, Unity has the number ten, and the number ten has Unity.

53. **Tat.** O Father, I now see the Universe, and myself in the Mind <note>original: "Minde"; referring to the Divine Intellect or *Nous*, which allows for spiritual perception beyond the physical senses</note>.

54. **Herm.** This is **Regeneration**, O Son: that we should no longer fix our imagination upon this body, which is subject to the three dimensions. According to the discourse we have just discussed, we do this so that we may not disparage the Universe at all <note>original: "calumniate the Universe"; to slander or speak ill of the world. Hermes argues that once reborn, a person sees the divine order in the world rather than viewing it as a prison of darkness</note>:

<summary>Hermes explains that the "ten powers" of divine virtue lead the soul back to unity and spiritual life. Tat experiences a vision of himself within the Divine Mind, and Hermes defines regeneration as the shift from focusing on the physical body to understanding the divine nature of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, the Decad, Unity, Regeneration, Divine Mind, three dimensions, spiritual rebirth</keywords>

# THE Eighth Book

### OF

## Hermes Trismegistus.

# That the greatest Evil in Man is the not knowing of God.

Where are you being carried, O men, you who are drunk from drinking the strong wine of ignorance? Since you cannot handle it, why do you not vomit it up again?

2. Stop and be sober, and look up again with the eyes of your heart <note>original: "eyes of your heart." This is a common mystical and biblical metaphor referring to spiritual perception or intuition rather than physical sight.</note>; and if you cannot

<summary>In this opening of the Eighth Book, Hermes issues a stern call to humanity to wake up from the "drunkenness" of spiritual ignorance. He urges people to regain their sobriety and use their inner spiritual vision to seek God.</summary>

### THE
# Ninth Book
### OF
### Hermes Trismegistus.
## *A Universal Ser-*
## *mon to Asclepius*

Hermes. All that is moved, O **Asclepius**, is it not moved in something, and by something?

2. **Asclepius.** Yes, indeed.
3. **Hermes.** Must not that in which a thing is moved necessarily <note>original: "of necessity"</note> be greater than the thing that is moved?
4. Of necessity.
5. And that which moves, is it not stronger than that which is moved?

<meta>catchword: 6. Asclepius.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Ninth Book of the Hermetica, beginning a Socratic-style dialogue between Hermes and Asclepius on the fundamental laws of motion and space.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, motion, space, philosophy, Hermetica, universal sermon</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes and Asclepius from the beginning of Book Nine, exploring the relationship between an object in motion and the space that contains it.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>The ninth Book of</header>

6. **Asclepius.** It is stronger.

7. **Hermes.** That in which a thing is moved—must it not necessarily <note>original: "must it not needs"</note> have a nature contrary to that of the thing being moved?

8. **Asclepius.** It must necessarily.

9. **Hermes.** Is not this great world a body, and is there any body greater than it?

10. **Asclepius.** Yes, admittedly.

11. **Hermes.** And is it not solid, as it is filled with many great bodies—and indeed, with all the bodies that exist?

12. **Asclepius.** It is so.

13. **Hermes.** And is not the world a body, and a body that is in motion <note>original: "moved"</note>?

14. **Asclepius.** It is.

15. **Hermes.** Then what kind of place must it be in which the world moves, and of what nature must that place be? Must it not be much larger, so that it may accommodate the continuous nature of its motion <note>original: "receive the continuity of motion"</note>? And so that...

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Hermes leads Asclepius through a logical proof regarding the nature of the universe, arguing that the space containing the world must be larger than the world itself and possess a different nature to allow for continuous motion.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, cosmology, laws of motion, physics, the world as a body, space</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes and Asclepius from page 120, where they discuss the nature of the space that contains the moving universe.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

that the thing being moved should be stopped and hindered in its motion for lack of room?

16. **Asclepius.** It must necessarily <note>original: "must needs"</note> be an immense thing, **Trismegistus**; but of what nature?

17. **Hermes.** Of a contrary nature, O **Asclepius**. But is not the nature of things that are incorporeal <note>original: "unbodily"</note> contrary to that of a body?

18. **Asclepius.** Admittedly <note>original: "Confessedly"</note>.

19. **Hermes.** Therefore, the place is incorporeal. But that which is incorporeal is either something divine or God himself. And by "something divine," I do not mean something that was created or born <note>original: "made or begotten"</note>.

20. If, therefore, it is divine, it is an essence or substance; but if it is God, he is above essence. Yet he is understandable <note>original: "intelligible"</note> in a different way.

**Trismegistus**, are those things that are here moved along with the things that move them? For you say that the spheres that wander <note>the planets</note> are moved by the sphere that does not wander <note>the celestial sphere of fixed stars</note>.

27. **Herm.** That, O **Asclepius**, is not a simultaneous motion, but a counter-motion; for they are not moved in the same manner, but in opposition to one another. And this opposition has a standing resistance to motion, for <term>resistance <note>original Greek: ἀντιτυπία (antitypia)</note></term> is a staying or stabilizing of motion.

28. Therefore, the wandering spheres, being moved in opposition to that sphere which does not wander, shall have a mutual opposition that stands by itself.

# Tenth Book

## Hermes Trismegistus.

->*The Mind to Hermes.*<-

Stop your speaking, O **Hermes Trismegistus**, and remember those things that have been said. I will not delay in speaking what comes to my mind, since <note>original: *sithence*</note> many men have spoken many things—and those very different from one another—concerning the Universe and the Good, but I have not yet learned the Truth.

2. Therefore, may the Lord <note>Referring to the Divine Mind or Supreme Being</note> make it plain to me on this point; for

<meta>catchword: I.</meta>

I will believe you only, for the revelation <note>original: manifestation</note> of these things.

3. Then the Mind said, "This is how the case stands:

4. God and All.

5. God, Eternity, the World, Time, Generation <note>The process of birth and coming into being in the material world</note>.

6. God made Eternity, Eternity made the World, the World made Time, and Time made Generation.

---

7. From God—as if from His very essence <note>original: substance</note>—come the **Good**, **Beautiful Blessedness**, and **Wisdom**.

8. From Eternity come Identity, or Selfhood.

9. From the World comes Order.

10. From Time comes Change.

11. From Generation come Life and Death.

12. But the activity <note>original: operation</note> of God is the Mind and the Soul.

13. From Eternity come Permanence (or endurance) and Immortality.

61. And you do not need to learn from me, O beloved **Hermes**, that all these things are created.

62. For they are **Bodies** <note>In this context, "Bodies" refers to physical or celestial entities, such as planets and stars.</note>, and they have a **Soul**, and they are moved.

65. For seeing that the motions are diverse and many, and the **Bodies** are not alike, and yet there is one ordered **speed** <note>original: "swiftnesse"</note> among them all, it is impossible that there should be two or more **Creators** <note>original: "Makers"</note>.

132. For the greatest evil is not knowing God.

### THE
# Eleventh Book
### OF
## *Hermes Trismegistus.*
### Of the Common Mind to Tat.

1. The mind, O **Tat** <note>Tat is the son and disciple of Hermes Trismegistus in these dialogues.</note>, is of the very essence of God—if indeed there is any essence of God <note>original: "Essence"; the fundamental nature or substance of a thing.</note>.

2. What kind of essence that is, He alone knows exactly.

3. The mind, therefore, is not cut off or divided from the essential nature of God, but is united to it just as light is united to the sun.

4. And this Mind in men is God; and for that reason, some men

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<summary>Hermes begins a new discourse to his son Tat, explaining that the human mind is not separate from God but is an extension of the Divine essence, much like sunlight is an extension of the sun.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Essence of God, Universal Mind, Divine Nature, spiritual unity, emanation</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the Eleventh Book of the Divine Pymander, "Of the Common Mind to Tat," following the assertion that the human mind is essentially divine.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>The eleventh Book of</header>

are called divine men, and there Humanity is near Divinity.

5. For the good **Spirit** <note>original: "Demon"; referring to the *Agathos Daimon*, a teacher of wisdom in Hermetic tradition.</note> called the gods "immortal men," and men "mortal gods."

6. But in brute beasts, or irrational living creatures <term>original: "wights"; an archaic term for beings or creatures.</term>, the Mind is simply their nature.

7. For where there is a soul, there is the Mind; just as where there is life, there is also a soul.

8. In living creatures, therefore, that are without reason, the soul is life itself, devoid of the operations of the Mind.

9. For the Mind is the benefactor of the souls of men and works toward their proper Good.

10. And in irrational things, it cooperates with the nature of every one of them, but in men, it works against their animal natures. <note>The author suggests that while the Mind aligns with the instincts of animals, it often challenges or suppresses human impulses in favor of spiritual growth.</note>

<vocab>Spirit, Gods, Beasts, Mind, Soul, Life, Reason, Nature, Humanity, Divinity</vocab>

<meta>catchword: 11. For</meta>

<summary>Hermes explains the distinction between the roles of the Mind in humans and animals, defining gods as immortal men and men as mortal gods. He argues that while the Mind is present in all living things, it serves as a moral guide in humans by working against their lower instincts.</summary>
<keywords>Agathos Daimon, immortal men, mortal gods, irrational creatures, nature vs. mind, soul and life, divine benefactor</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the Eleventh Book of the Divine Pymander, "Of the Common Mind to Tat." The previous page established that the Mind acts as a benefactor to human souls, often working against their lower, animal instincts to guide them toward the Good.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

11. For the soul, being in the body, is immediately made evil by sorrow, grief, and pleasure or delight.

12. For grief and pleasure flow like juices <note>The author uses "juices" to describe the "humors" or bodily fluids that were thought to dictate a person's physical and emotional state.</note> from the composite body; when the soul enters or descends into the body, she is moistened and stained <note>original: "tincted"</note> with them.

13. Therefore, for as many souls as the Mind governs or rules over, it shows its own light to them, resisting their internal biases <note>original: "prepossessions"</note> or false assumptions.

96. For it is impossible that anything which brings forth <note>original: "bringeth forth"; gives birth or produces life</note> should do so without motion.

97. For the word "immovable," or "without motion," signifies nothing else but idleness.

98. Understand this as a general rule, my son: whatever exists in the world is moved either by growth <term>augmentation</term> or by shrinking <term>diminution</term>.

99. Furthermore, that which is moved is also alive. Yet, it is not necessary for a living thing to remain exactly the same.

100. For while the world as a whole remains together, it is unchangeable, my son; however, all of its individual parts are subject to change.

101. Yet nothing is truly corrupted <note>original: "corrupted"; here meaning the physical decay or breaking down of a substance</note>, destroyed, or completely abolished.

### THE
# Twelfth Book

### *His Crater or Monas.*
<note>The "Crater" (from the Greek *krater*) refers to a large mixing bowl or vessel, symbolizing a vat of divine Mind into which souls are dipped. "Monas" refers to the "Monad," the indivisible One or the ultimate source of all existence.</note>

1. The Creator <note>original: "Workman"; a translation of the Greek *Demiurgos*, the craftsman or divine architect of the universe.</note> made this universal world, not with his hands, but with his Word <note>original: "Word"; here referring to the *Logos*, the divine reason or creative command.</note>.
2. Therefore, think of him in this way: as being present everywhere, existing eternally, and creating all things; he is the One above who, by his Will, has framed everything that exists.
3. For that is his Body: it is not tangible, nor visible, nor measurable, nor capable of extension <note>original: "extensible"</note>, nor is it like any other body.

# THE Thirteenth Book
## OF Hermes Trismegistus.
### *Of Sense and Understanding.*

1. Yesterday, *Asclepius*, I delivered a complete *discourse* <note>original: "perfect Discourse"; likely referring to the teachings in the preceding book</note>; but now I think it is necessary, as a sequel to that, to also discuss *Sense*.

2. For *Sense* and *Understanding* seem to differ, because the one is material and the other is essential <term>essential: belonging to the "essence" or spiritual substance of a being, rather than the physical body</term>.

3. But to me, they appear to be both one, or united and not divided—in men, I mean.

4. For in other living *creatures*, *Sense* is united to *Nature*, but in men it is united to *Understanding*.

<meta>catchword: 5. But</meta>

<summary>Hermes begins a new lesson for Asclepius, arguing that while sense and understanding appear different, they are unified in humans. He distinguishes humans from other animals by noting that human sensation is tied to the intellect rather than just biological nature.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, Sense, Understanding, Human Nature, Animal Nature, Material vs. Essential, Hermetic Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the Thirteenth Book of Hermes Trismegistus, "Of Sense and Understanding," following the previous page's introduction that human sensation is uniquely tied to the intellect.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus. 195</header>

5. But the Mind <note>original: "Mind" here likely refers to the higher divine intellect (*Nous*)</note> differs from Understanding as much as God differs from Divinity.

6. For Divinity is under <note>original Greek: *hypo* (ὑπὸ), meaning "from" or "under"</note> God, and Understanding comes from the Mind, being the sister of the Word <note>original: "the Word" refers to *Logos*, meaning both speech and the divine reason</note> or Speech; and they are the instruments of one another.

7. For the Word is not pronounced without Understanding, nor is Understanding manifested without the Word.

8. Therefore, Sense and Understanding both flow together into a person, as if they were folded one within another.

9. For it is neither possible to understand without Sense, nor can we have Sense without Understanding.

10. And yet it is possible—for the time being—that the Understanding may understand without— <note>The text continues on the next page, likely reading "without Sense."</note>

<sig>K 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: without-</meta>

<summary>Hermes explains the hierarchical relationship between God, the Mind, and Understanding, arguing that human sensation and intellect are inextricably linked. He posits that while Divinity flows from God, Understanding flows from the Mind and works in tandem with speech and sensation.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mind, Nous, Understanding, Divinity, Logos, The Word, Sensation, Human Intellect, Hermetic Philosophy</keywords>

...without Sense, as those who imagine visions in their dreams.

11. But it seems to me that both operations <note>The "operations" of Sense and Understanding</note> are present in the visions of dreams, and that Sense is stirred up from sleep into awakening.

### THE
# Fourteenth Book
### OF
## *Hermes Trismegistus.*
### *of Operation and Sense.*

**Tat.** You have well explained these things, Father. Teach me furthermore about these matters; for you say that **Science and Art** <note>original: "Science & Art"; in this context, "Science" refers to systematic knowledge or "Scientia," while "Art" refers to technical skill or "Ars"</note> were the operations <note>term: Operations — the active functions or workings of the soul and mind</note> of the rational mind. But now you say that beasts are unreasonable, and for want of reason, they both are, and are called, brutes <note>original: "Bruits" — irrational animals lacking the capacity for abstract thought</note>. So, by this reasoning, it must necessarily follow that unreasonable creatures do not partake in **Science** or **Art**, because they...

<meta>The text cuts off at the catchword "they," which will lead into the next page's explanation of the limitations of animal perception compared to human intellect.</meta>

<summary>In the opening of the Fourteenth Book, Tat asks Hermes to clarify why animals, being "brutes" without reason, are excluded from the "Sciences and Arts" that characterize human operation.</summary>

<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

1. ...they fall short of reason.
2. **Herm.** It must necessarily be so, my son.
3. **Tat.** Why then, O Father, do we see some irrational living creatures use both science and art? For example, **ants** <note>original: "Pismires"</note> store up food for themselves against the winter, and the birds of the air likewise make nests for themselves, and four-footed beasts know their own dens.
4. **Herm.** They do these things, O son, not by science or art, but by nature. For science and art are things that are taught, but none of these brute <note>original: "bruit" — irrational animals</note> beasts are taught any of these things.
5. But these things, being natural to them, are produced by nature; whereas art and science do not happen to everyone, but only to some.
6. Just as some men are musicians, but <note>The text continues on the next page with the catchword "not," likely explaining that while some men have specific arts, not all men do, unlike natural instincts which are common to a whole species.</note>

He distinguishes between innate nature and acquired skills, which are not universal to all individuals.</summary>

not all; neither are all archers, or hunters, or the rest; rather, some of them have learned these things through the application of science or art.

7. In the same way also, if some **ants** <note>original: "Pismires"</note> did so and others did not, you might well say they gather their food according to science and art.

8. But since they are all led by nature to do the same thing, even without their own choice <note>original: "against their wills"</note>, it is clear they do not do it by science or art.

9. For operations <note>original: "Operations" — here referring to the active forces or functions of nature</note>, O **Tat**, being incorporeal <note>original: "unbodily"</note>, exist within bodies and work through bodies.

10. Therefore, O **Tat**, insofar as they are incorporeal, you must say they are immortal.

11. But because they cannot act without bodies, I say they are always within a body.

12. For those things that are made subject to Providence <note>divine foresight and the celestial order of the universe</note> or Necessity for any reason or cause, cannot possibly remain idle or cease their own proper operation <note>original: "Operation" — here referring to the active, natural function or energy of a thing</note>.

13. For that which exists shall always exist; for both the body and the life within it are the same <note>meaning they are part of the same continuous cycle of existence</note>.

14. And by this reasoning, it follows that bodies also are always present; because I affirm that this corporeity <note>physical existence or the state of having a body</note> exists always through, or for the sake of, the act and operation.

15. For although earthly bodies are subject to dissolution <note>breaking down or decaying</note>, these bodies must still be the places, the organs, and the instruments of acts or operations.

16. But acts or operations are immortal, and that which is immortal is always active; and therefore, **corporification** <note>the process of taking on or creating a physical body</note> must also be perpetual.

<meta>catchword: 17. Acts</meta>

<summary>Hermes argues that because natural forces (operations) are immortal and require physical forms to express themselves, the existence of bodies and the process of taking on a physical form (corporification) must be eternal.</summary>

<header>The 14th Book of Hermes Trismegistus</header>

17. Acts or operations follow the soul, yet they do not arrive suddenly or randomly <note>original: "promiscuously"</note>. Instead, some of them appear as soon as a person is created, dealing with animalistic <note>original: "bruitish"</note> or irrational things.

18. But the purer operations work gradually over time, functioning with the rational <note>original: "oblique" — likely referring to the higher, indirect path of the intellect compared to direct sensory instinct</note> part of the soul.

19. And these operations depend upon bodies; truly, those that are **embodiment-forming** <note>original: "Corporifying" — the process of giving physical form to something</note> come from divine bodies into mortal ones.

20. Every one of them acts upon both the body and the soul, and they remain present with the soul even without the body.

21. They are always acts or operations, but the soul is not always in a mortal body; it can exist without a body. However, acts or operations 

<meta>catchword: cannot</meta>

cannot exist <note>original: "be"</note> without bodies.

22. This is a sacred teaching <note>original: "speech"</note>, my son: **The Body cannot exist <note>original: "consist"</note> without a Soul.**

23. **Tat:** What do you mean by that, Father?

24. **Hermes:** Understand it this way, O **Tat**: when the soul is separated from the body, that same body remains.

25. And this same body, throughout the duration of its existence <note>original: "according to the time of its abode"</note>, is acted upon <note>original: "actuated or operated"</note> so that it dissolves and becomes invisible.

26. And the body cannot undergo <note>original: "suffer"</note> these things without some action or process <note>original: "act or operation"</note>; and consequently, that same action or process remains with the body.

27. This, then, is the difference between an immortal body and a mortal one: the immortal body consists of one single kind of matter, but the mortal body does not.

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that while a soul can exist without a mortal body, a body requires the presence of "operations"—active forces or processes—even during the process of decay and dissolution after death. He defines the difference between mortal and immortal bodies based on the simplicity or complexity of their constituent matter.</summary>

# THE Fifteenth Book
## OF
## *Hermes Trismegistus.*
### *Concerning Truth: To his Son Tat.*

1. **Hermes:** Regarding Truth, O **Tat**, it is not possible for man to speak with any certainty <note>original: "confidence"</note>. For man is an imperfect creature <note>original: "wight"</note>, composed of imperfect parts; his physical vessel <note>original: "Tabernacle," a biblical metaphor for the human body</note> consists of many different types of matter <note>original: "Bodies"</note>.

2. But as far as it is possible and right, I say that Truth is only in the Eternal Bodies <note>referring to celestial or divine entities</note>, whose very forms are also true.

3. Fire is fire itself only, and nothing else; Earth is earth itself, and nothing else;

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Hermes introduces the concept of absolute Truth, explaining that because humans are complex and imperfect physical beings, they cannot possess the pure Truth found in eternal and elemental bodies.</summary>

5. And if our very nature <note>original: "Constitution"</note> did not possess Truth from its beginning, how could humans either see the Truth, speak it, or even understand it, unless God willed it so?

6. Therefore, O **Tat**, all things on Earth are not the Truth itself, but are imitations of the Truth. And yet, not even all things are imitations; there are only a few things that are truly so.

7. But other things are falsehood and deceit, O **Tat**, and mere opinions which are like images of the imagination <note>original: "fantasie"</note> or outward appearance.

## THE
# Sixteenth Book
## OF
### Hermes Trismegistus.

->*That none of the things that exist can perish.*<-

Hermes: We must now speak of the soul and the body, my son; in what way the soul is immortal, and what process <note>original: "operation"; the functional mechanism or divine action.</note> it is that creates the body and later dissolves it.

<summary>Hermes introduces a discussion on the relationship between the soul and the body, asserting that "death" is an empty or misunderstood term for the natural transformation of things.</summary>

5. But all things that are in the world are members of the world, especially Man, the rational living being.

# The Seventeenth Book
### OF
## Hermes Trismegistus.

### To *Asclepius*, to be truly *wise*.

Because my son **Tat**, in your absence, insisted on learning the nature of the things that exist, he would not allow me to stop—since he came so young to the knowledge of every individual thing <note>original: "Individuall"; here referring to the specific components or distinct entities of the universe.</note>—until I was forced to discuss many things with him at length, so that his contemplation might, point by point, be easier and more successful.

2. But for you, I have thought it best to write briefly, choosing the main points <note>original: "principall heads"</note> of the things spoken of then, and to interpret—

<summary>In this introduction to the seventeenth book, Hermes explains that he is providing Asclepius with a concise summary of the complex metaphysical lessons he recently taught his son, Tat.</summary>

...interpret them more mystically, because you have both more years and more knowledge of Nature.

3. All things that appear <note>original: "appeare"; referring to the visible or manifest world.</note> were made and are still being made.

4. Those things that are made are not made by themselves, but by another.

5. And there are many things made, especially all things that appear, which are different and not alike.

6. If the things that are made and done are made and done by another, there must be one who makes and does them; and he is unmade <note>original: "unmade"; meaning uncreated or self-existent.</note> and more ancient than the things that are made.

7. For I affirm that the things that are made are made by another; and it is impossible that, of the things that are made, any should be more ancient than all the others, except only for that which is not made.

### THE
# Second Book
### OF
## *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>original: "Hermes Trismegistus," or "Thrice-Greatest Hermes," a legendary Hellenistic figure who represents a combination of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with writing these sacred texts.</note>.

## CHAPTER 1.

You, *Asclepius* <note>original: "Asclepius," the Greek god of healing, here portrayed as a student or companion receiving the divine wisdom of Hermes.</note>, serve instead of a sun to me; for God has brought you to us, so that you might be present with us in your divine *discourse*. This teaching is of such a kind that it may seem worthy to carry a greater luster of *piety* and *religion* than all the works we have previously done, or any gifts inspired by divine *inspiration*. If you regard this with understanding, you shall be richly filled with all

<meta>catchword: good</meta>

<summary>In this opening chapter of the Second Book, Hermes welcomes his student Asclepius, comparing his presence to the sun and introducing a divine discourse on piety and religion.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, divinity, piety, religious discourse, divine inspiration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Second Book. The previous page ended with the promise that the reader would be "richly filled with all..."</meta>

<header>The second Book of</header>

good things throughout your whole soul. If, nevertheless, there are many good things and not one general source in which all things exist—for each is perceived to consent and agree with the other—then all these things belong to that One, and that One is All. For the one so coheres to the other that they cannot be separated. But in the coming discourse, by listening diligently, you shall fully know it.

But you, O **Asclepius**, step forward a little and call for him who should be present. Upon entering, **Asclepius** also suggests that **Ammon** <note>original: "Amnon," a variant spelling of Ammon, a figure often identified with the Egyptian god Amun, here serving as a witness to the divine teaching.</note> be present. 

**Trismegistus** says: "No envy keeps **Ammon** from us; for we remember many things written by us in his name, as also to his loving and dear son—many things concerning natural philosophy <note>original: "naturall Philosophy," the study of nature and the physical universe, which in this context includes the spiritual laws governing the cosmos.</note>, and of many..."

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding natural philosophy and outlandish, strange things. Hermes is preparing to deliver a sacred teaching to a select, private group.</meta>

<header># Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

...outlandish and strange things. But I will dedicate this treatise <note>original: "Tractate," a formal written work dealing with a specific subject</note> to your name, and I will call no one else except **Ammon**, lest a most holy discourse on such a weighty matter be violated by the interference and presence of many outsiders. For it is evidence of an insincere and irreligious heart to expose a treatise filled with the fullness of the divine Majesty to the common gaze. 

Holy **Ammon** having now entered into a private room <note>original: "closet," meaning a small room used for private prayer, study, or confidential meetings</note>, prepared by the devotion of four men and the Divine Presence of God, he begins with great reverence and secrecy to declare himself. In the name of all the followers of Hermes <note>original: "Hermestes"</note>, he speaks to the minds and souls of those who are prepared to listen.

*This first chapter teaches <note>original: "teacheth"</note> that all things belong to one, and that all things are one, from one, as that from which all things are, One; just as all the effects which in their cause are one, and that every man's soul is immortal—but yet after a different sort. These are one Body, one Soul, one World, and one God. ### CHAPTER II.

Heaven, therefore, is the perceptible <note>original: "sensible," meaning that which can be perceived by the senses rather than just the intellect</note> preserver of all those bodies whose growth and decline are governed, as it were, by the power of the Sun and Moon. But God, who is the Creator of all things, is the Governor of Heaven, of its Soul, and of all things in the world. For from all the aforementioned things—all of which have a Governor—there is a constant influence carried through the world by nature itself, and by the soul of every genus <note>original: "Genus," referring to a general category or classification of being</note> and species <note>original: "Species," referring to the specific forms within a genus</note> within it. ...a receptacle for every sort of species or form. By shaping nature through these forms, He has brought the world—through the four elements—even up to heaven. All of God’s works that are pleasing to the eye and that hang above us are divided into species, and I am now about to describe them in that way.

The **genera** <note>original: "Genera," the plural of genus, meaning broad categories or classifications of being</note>, or kinds, of all things follow their **species** <note>original: "Species," referring to the specific forms within a genus</note>, because the **genus** is the totality or the substance of a thing, and the **species** is a part of that **genus**. Therefore, there is a **genus** of good spirits and a **genus** of bad ones; the same is true for humans, birds, and all things the world has. The world produces **species** that resemble itself. There is another **genus** of irrational animals <note>original: "brute Beasts"</note> that indeed lack understanding and reason, but they are not without a soul or life. <meta>This page continues the commentary on the definitions used by Hermes Trismegistus, specifically addressing how his terminology for "soul" and "living beings" differs from standard Early Modern or Aristotelian definitions.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

[As] shall be said, this suggests that *Mercury* <note>original: "Mercurius"; the Roman name for the Greek god Hermes, associated here with Hermes Trismegistus</note> uses the word *Animal* in a much different sense than we are accustomed to, as well as the word *Soul* <note>original Latin: "Anima"</note>. For in the second dialogue of the *Pimander* <note>The *Pimander* (or Poimandres) is a foundational text of Hermeticism, focusing on creation and the nature of the soul</note>, he defines the *Soul* by motion. Therefore, according to his way of speaking, anything that has the faculty of movement has a *Soul* <note>original: "Animam"</note>; and whatever has both a *Soul* and a *Body* is an *Animal* <note>In this context, *Animal* comes from the Latin *animalis*, meaning "animated" or "endowed with the breath of life," rather than just referring to a non-human creature</note>.

The *Heavens*, therefore, are an *Animal*; and likewise the world, plants, and the elements are "animals." However, it is our custom to only call something an *Animal* if it is a living *creature* and has senses. We define the *Soul* (*Anima*) not just by motion, but by *life*, *sense*, *voluntary motion*, and *understanding*. Therefore, when we hear the word *Animal*, let us understand it in his sense and not in our own. But now, returning to the dialogue: he compares the *Heavens* and *heavenly* bodies to other *sensible* <note>meaning things that can be perceived by the senses</note> things

<summary>This page explains that Hermes Trismegistus defines "Animal" and "Soul" based on the presence of motion, meaning that he considers the heavens, the world, and even plants to be "animals" because they possess a soul-driven movement.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mercurius, Anima, Animal, Soul, Pimander, motion, cosmic hierarchy, definitions, Hermeticism</keywords>

## CHAPTER III.

In this regard, O Asclepius <note>Asclepius is the disciple to whom Hermes Trismegistus is speaking in this dialogue; he is often associated with the Greek god of medicine.</note>, man is a great miracle—a creature both to be reverenced and honored. He is made according to the nature and image of God, as though he were a god himself. The angels know this, because they were created with the same nature; however, they disdained the human part of that nature and relied only on the divine nature.

Therefore, the more temperate <note>In this context, "temperate" refers to a well-ordered or balanced soul that is not ruled by physical passions.</note> the nature of man is, and the closer it comes to God and to divinity, the more he despises that part of himself which makes him earthly. a physical and earthly enclosure <note>original: "corporeall and earthy closure"</note>; and that the will of God is the ultimate perfection of all things. Necessity follows this will, and the physical result <note>original: "effect"</note> follows that necessity; for God fulfilled both his will and his action in one and the same moment of time. 

The fact that he calls the world the "second Deity" is the same as if you were to call it a second duality <note>original: "duetie"</note> and unity. For while two is one and one is two, "one" is absolutely one, whereas "two" is not absolutely one, but becomes one through **participation** and **contraction** <note>participation and contraction: philosophical terms describing how a manifold or diverse thing shares in the nature of the "One" or is narrowed down from a higher spiritual state</note>. Thus, there is only one absolute God, and the world is not God, but is "God" through participation, being the very template <note>original: "stamp"</note> of all perceptible and delightful things. This concludes the commentary for the fourth part of *Asclepius*.

## CHAPTER V.

But, O *Asclepius*, I observe that you earnestly desire to hear how a person <note>original: "a man"</note> may come to enjoy that musical harmony and divine worship which belongs to heaven. For God is only pleased and delighted when humans extol <note>extol: to praise highly</note> His admirable work, sing praises of thanksgiving to Him, and perform the worship and service that belongs to His holy name. Nor do those heavenly graces descend unworthily into the congregation—

## COMMENTARY.

*This sixth chapter discusses* <note>original: "discourseth"</note> *that God is the first God in an absolute sense; the World is the second—not in an absolute sense, but by participating in God—serving as the first Image of an absolute deity. Man is the third God by participation in the divinity, and the second Image of God. But God is not an Image; rather, he is the reality* <note>original: "truth"</note> *behind all Images, so that man may ascend up into heaven by his soul, by his understanding, by his spirit, and by his reason, as if by the Superior Elements* <note>In Hermetic thought, these are the spiritual components of a human that correspond to the celestial spheres</note>. *But because man is also made of fire, air, water, and earth, he is subject to death and prone to obsession with* <note>original: "dote upon"</note> *all worldly things when he is deprived of that divine part. The standard* <note>original: "measure"</note> *by which a man ought to live is religion, which goodness follows, and which seems to...*

## CHAPTER VII.

I will now begin to speak of the Spirit and similar matters. There was **God** and ***Hyle*** <note>original: "Hyle"; from the Greek *hylē* (ὕλη). While the text here says it means "the world," it more accurately refers to "matter" or the raw substance of the universe</note>, and the Spirit was in the world. However, the Spirit was not in the world in the same way it is with God. Neither are those things which the world is made of God Himself. Therefore, they did not exist when they had not yet been created; yet even then, they existed within that source from which they received their being. 

For things are not only said to "be" when they have not yet been created, but also when they lack fruitfulness in generation <note>original: "generating"; the ability to produce or give birth to something new</note>, so that nothing can be produced or generated from them. Therefore, whatever things have within them the nature of conceiving, those are

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 7, defining the relationship between God, the Spirit, and "Hyle" (matter/the world). It argues that created things existed in potential within their source before their actual creation.</summary>

For a beginning exists in the quality of nature, which contains within itself the quality and nature of conception and birth. ## The COMMENTARY.

The seventh chapter contains a hidden philosophy regarding the creation <note>original: "generation"</note> and the physical matter of the world, as well as the spirit that accompanies it and the nature of space. He calls the world within God the "containing place" <note>original: "Continent place"; from the Latin 'continens', meaning that which holds or contains everything else</note>. For this reason, he does not consider the space of the world to have been created in the usual sense. Instead, he views it as both created and uncreated: it is indeed created if you consider...

<summary>Hermes explains that divine reason elevates exceptional minds to a god-like status, followed by a commentary exploring the Hermetic view of the universe's creation and the paradox of space as both created and eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Divine Reason, intellect, creation, Continent place, cosmology, Hermeticism, Spirit of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the nature of creation, exploring how the physical world and the spirit existed within the divine Word before their physical manifestation. It also begins to address the dual nature of the world, which contains the potential for both good and evil.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>The Second Book of</header>

*...the creation of all living beings <note>original: "Creatures"</note>, and not created if you consider the true nature of that divine workmanship.* For the world and the spirit—namely, the world and a certain divine power passing through and spreading itself through all things—did not exist in the physical world before they were created. Instead, he says they existed within the source from which they were to be created. And what was that source, if not that divine Word <note>original: "word"; likely referring to the Logos</note> through which all things were made? 

Within that source, and within what was made, was life; and it is from there that everything that was created eventually came. The world is as productive of evil as it is of good, being equally capable of both, just as the Earth is productive of both medicinal herbs and harmful weeds. Just as the Earth possesses this quality by its own inherent nature <note>original: "propriety"; from the Latin 'proprietas', meaning a characteristic or essential quality</note>, so too does the world—even though God (as much as was reasonably possible) has provided for human rea-

<keywords>creation, divine spirit, Word, Logos, nature of evil, dualism, Hermes Trismegistus, cosmology</keywords>

...which I now begin by the desired help and assistance of God. 

There are diverse kinds of gods, and of those, one part is intelligible <note>apprehended by the intellect or mind rather than physical senses</note> and another part is sensible <note>perceived through the physical senses</note>. They are called "intelligibles," not because they are thought to be beyond the reach of our senses (for we actually perceive them more than those which we call "visibles"), as our discourse shall show, and as you may perceive yourself if you pay attention. 

For divine reason—which passes beyond the reach and capacity of men—will fly away, pass through you, and return to the fountain of its own waters if you do not listen to the speakers with great care and intense attention. 

There are, therefore, chief lords or gods of all species <note>original: "Species"; referring to the essential forms or categories of being</note>, the prince of whom is **Usia** <note>original Greek: *Ousia*, meaning "Essence" or "Substance"</note>. These are like one another in their origin; they, by their nature, effect all things, every...

**Asclepius.** Again, what is the reason for this, O **Trismegistus**?

**Trismegistus.** This, O **Asclepius**: For God the Father, or the Lord of all things—or by whatever name he is more religiously and devoutly called by men—ought to be reverenced by us in contemplation of so great a Majesty for the sake of our own understanding. <header>The second Book of</header>

Is the name of God then complete or whole in these syllables? For I do not believe that the primary cause <note>original: "efficient cause"</note> of all majesty, and the Father and Lord of all things, can be expressed by a single name, even if it were made up of many syllables. It is necessary, therefore, that he should be called not by just one name, but rather by all names (since he is both the One and all things). It is appropriate <note>original: "meet"</note> that all things should be his name, or that he should be titled by the name of "The All." 

This Being, therefore—who is one because he is all—is full of the fruitfulness of both natures <note>A reference to the Hermetic belief that the Divine is both masculine and feminine, or possesses both creative and receptive powers.</note>. Rich in his own will, he brings forth whatever he has a desire to create. His will is entirely goodness, and this same goodness belongs to all things. From his divinity, nature proceeds so that all things may be as they are,

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus argues that because God is the source of everything, he cannot be captured by a single name but must be addressed by all names or as "The All." He describes the Divine as a fruitful, dual-natured being whose will is pure goodness and the origin of nature itself.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, God, Divine Names, The All, creation, efficient cause, goodness, nature, fruitfulness</keywords>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The second Book of</header>

God assigned them for the purpose and hope of immortality. Finally, he has made man so that he might be immortal, possessing both a divine and a mortal nature. This was done so it might appear that man—being created this way by the will of God—is in a better state than the angels (who are made only of an immortal nature) and superior to all other mortal creatures. 

In this respect, as man comes nearest to the nature of God, he should worship Him in pure and undefiled religion with an honest heart. Likewise, the angels, with a pious and loving affection, should pity and protect all human affairs. 

However, this is only spoken concerning those few men who are endowed <note>original: "indued"</note> with chaste and pure affections. We have nothing to do with the vicious <note>original: "Vitious"; those characterized by vice or wickedness</note>, lest this holy discourse be defiled by the thought of them.
