<header>Hermes Trismegistus. 67</header>

## The COMMENTARY.

This eighth chapter treats the material Lords or Princes, whom he calls Gods. The Lord of Heaven he calls *Jupiter*; the Lord of the Sun, the Light. He reckons thirty-four Horoscopes, or markers of the hours of children’s births <note>original: "nativity"</note>, in the same location as the fixed planets. The chief of these he calls *Pantomorphos* <note>original: "Pantomorphos"; a Greek term meaning "all-formed" or "having all shapes"</note>. He designates *Fortune* and *Fate* as the Lords of the seven wandering spheres, or planets. He also appoints a Lord of the air. 

However, these seem to be the fictions of demons <note>original: "Devils"</note> and certain vanities that suggest the errors of the Gentiles <note>term: "Gentiles"; here referring to the ancient Greeks, Romans, and other non-Christian peoples whose polytheistic or astrological beliefs the author views as spiritually dangerous</note>. Such beliefs cause those who trust in them to perish, rather than to be in any safety. From here, he returns to the contemplation of the most high and ineffable <note>term: "ineffable"; something too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words</note>—

<summary>This page critiques the eighth chapter of Hermes' work, which describes various "material lords" and astrological entities like Jupiter and the 34 horoscopes. The commentator dismisses these as demonic fictions and pagan errors that lead people astray from the true, ineffable God.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Jupiter, astrology, Horoscopes, Pantomorphos, Fortune, Fate, Gentiles, demons, ineffable</keywords>

**Asclepius**, consider the authority and power of man. Divinity will return from Earth back into Heaven; Egypt will be abandoned, and the land which was the seat of divinity will be devoid of religion and deprived of the presence of the Deity. For when foreigners <note>original: "strangers"</note> occupy and fill this land and kingdom, not only will religion be neglected; but, what is even more wretched, laws will be enacted against religion, piety, and divine worship, with punishments inflicted upon those who seem to support it. <meta>This page provides a critical commentary on the previous chapter's discussion of the Egyptian gods. While Hermes Trismegistus previously described a hierarchy of deities like Jupiter and Pluto-Jupiter governing the earth, this commentator uses Christian theology to reject those ideas as idolatrous.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>The second Book of</header>

->The<-
## COMMENTARY.

This entire ninth chapter is profane <note>original: "prophane"; meaning sacrilegious or irreverent toward God</note>. Saint **Augustine** correctly refutes this in his book, ***The City of God*** <note>original: *De Civitate Dei*; a famous work by Augustine of Hippo criticizing pagan beliefs and defending Christianity</note>. This chapter supports idolatry, exalting and promoting it with wonderful praise, while deeply mourning its decline and fall. In the highest heaven, the author places a certain God who watches over all things; but in the space between heaven and earth, he places **Jupiter** as the governor or steward. He then identifies **Pluto-Jupiter** as the god and ruler over the land and sea, as if there were one god in heaven, another in the air, and another on the land and sea. All of these ideas contain an infinite amount of impious errors. According to Holy Scripture <note>original: "divine Writ"</note>, we are taught in a pure, holy, and incorrupt way that the Lord alone is God.

<summary>The commentator rejects the Hermetic hierarchy of gods as pagan idolatry, citing Saint Augustine to argue that the division of divine rule among multiple deities is a sacrilegious error compared to the monotheism of the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, City of God, idolatry, Jupiter, Pluto-Jupiter, monotheism, paganism, Holy Scripture, theology</keywords>

--- Page 365 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological critique of the previous chapter. It transitions from refuting the Hermetic hierarchy of gods to citing specific biblical mandates against idolatry, specifically drawing from the Book of Wisdom and the Book of Leviticus to reinforce monotheism.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

God is in heaven above and on earth below, and there is no other God. Against idols and idolatry, the Word of God and the holy prophets earnestly <note>original: "warily"</note> warn us so that we are not defiled by such a great stain of wickedness <note>original: "impiety"</note>, nor corrupted by such a great error. For the idol itself is cursed, as is the one who made it. Furthermore, the worshipping of cursed idols is the cause, the beginning, and the end of all evil <note>original: "mischief"</note>. 

An idol maker and the idol itself are both an abomination to the Lord; for both the object that is made and the person who made it shall be consumed with fire. These and many more things regarding idolatry are found in the Book of Wisdom <note>A book of the Catholic and Orthodox Bibles, often included in the Apocrypha of Protestant Bibles during this period</note>. In the book of Leviticus, God himself commands with his own voice: "I am the Lord your God. You shall not make for yourselves any idol or carved <note>original: "graven"</note> image, neither shall you set up any monument or pillar in your land to worship..."

<summary>The author uses biblical citations from the Book of Wisdom and Leviticus to condemn idolatry, arguing that both idols and their creators are cursed and destined for destruction.</summary>
<keywords>idolatry, Book of Wisdom, Leviticus, monotheism, graven images, impiety, biblical law</keywords>

worship it. And regarding the idols of Egypt, he warns <note>original: "advertiseth"</note> in Ezekiel:

What Hermes <note>original: "Mercurius," the Roman name for Hermes Trismegistus</note> calls the foul spirit and the essence <note>original: "sense"</note> of idols and images—claiming they shall bring diseases, infirmities, and fears upon men—we know without doubt to be evil spirits. [To un]derstand is perfect righteousness, and to know His righteousness and power is the root of immortality. Lazzarelli <note>original: "Lazarelus"; likely Ludovico Lazzarelli (1447–1500), an Italian Hermeticist who sought to align Hermetic texts with Christian doctrine</note> draws this passage into an analogy, as though the idols were the Apostles; the image of man, Christ; the power given from above, the Holy Ghost; Egypt, the darkness of the Gentiles; and the persecution of the disciples, Apostles, and martyrs, the engraved pillars declaring their pious works—and that the hearts of posterity did retain not their works, but their faith only.

These are piously imagined, but perhaps far from the literal meaning or intent. I think, along with Augustine, that Hermes went too far <note>original: "overshot himself"</note> both in this and in the 13th chapter. For they seem to be like the prophets of the Gentiles, such as Balaam and the Sibyls <note>Balaam was a biblical non-Israelite prophet; the Sibyls were ancient pagan prophetesses often cited by early Christians as having foretold Christ</note>, so that they may experience both light and darkness—that mixture of clear and obscure prophecies that sometimes...

### CHAPTER 10

Now we will discuss mortality and immortality. The hope and fear of death torments many people who are ignorant of the true reason for it. Death is caused by the dissolution of the body when it is worn out with labor and its "harmony" <note>In Hermetic philosophy, health and life are often described as a musical or mathematical harmony between the elements of the body</note> has ended—the harmony by which the members of the body are joined into a single composition for the purposes of life. The body dies when the vital parts of a person fail. Therefore, death is simply a dissolution of the body and a complete decay of the physical senses; to worry about this is pointless.

<vocab>mortality, immortality, death, dissolution, body, harmony, vital parts, senses</vocab>

<summary>This page opens Chapter 10 with a philosophical definition of death, describing it as the natural dissolution of the body's physical harmony and the cessation of the senses.</summary>
<keywords>mortality, immortality, death, dissolution, harmony, vital parts, physical senses, Chapter 10</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Hermes Trismegistus, comparing the spiritual brilliance of a pious person to the Sun's light and explaining the Sun's role as a "Second God" that sustains life throughout the universe.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>Hermes Trismegistus.</header>

men, just as the Sun excels other stars in its light. For the Sun itself—not so much through the greatness of its deity <note>original: "diety," referring to its divine status or nature</note>, but through its divinity and holiness—illuminates the other stars. For I consider this Sun, O Asclepius, to be the Second God <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the Sun is often described as the "Second God" or the visible administrator of the invisible Creator's will</note> governing all other things and illuminating all worldly things, whether they are creatures with life or those without life. For if the world—which is itself a created being—has been, is, and shall always be living, then nothing in the world is truly mortal. There is no place for death <note>original: "mortality"</note>, because every living part within the world exists as part of one and the same continuous living creature <note>This refers to the *Anima Mundi* or "World Soul," the belief that the entire universe is a single, living, spiritual entity</note>. Therefore, it must be full of life and eternity if it is to live forever. The Sun, therefore, is as eternal as the world itself; it is the governor of life and vitality <note>original: "livelinesse"</note> and the continuous provider of them. He is, therefore, the God of the

## The COMMENTARY.

This tenth chapter speaks of death and the judgment <note>original: "examination"</note> of the soul when the body dies. It asserts that those whose lives are justly taken by human laws for the greatness of their offenses shall be punished most severely after death. It also teaches that God is a defense to the righteous, and it discusses the divinity of the Sun and the world. 

Hermes designates the world to be the "first sensible God" <note>original: "sensible," meaning a god that can be perceived by the senses or the physical world</note> and the Sun to be the "second sensible God." He claims that the world shall never rest nor be destroyed. However, we <note>The commentator is writing from a Christian perspective to contrast Hermes' pagan views with Christian doctrine.</note> do not give the incommunicable name of God to either the Sun or the world, and we believe that the world shall one day be dissolved. These things, as they are understood from the literal words of Hermes, reveal the errors of the Gentiles <note>original: "Gentiles," meaning the non-Christian or pagan people of antiquity</note>; yet—

<meta>catchword: we</meta>

<summary>This commentary discusses Hermes' views on the soul's judgment after death and his claim that the world and Sun are "sensible gods," while the author corrects these ideas from a Christian theological perspective.</summary>

## CHAP. XI.

Therefore, since all things are this way, among all created things—whether heavenly or earthly—nothing is stable, fixed, or immovable. For only God, and rightly He alone, exists in Himself and from Himself, and is entirely full and perfect in every way. This is His firm stability, which cannot be disturbed by the force or influence <note>original: "occasion"</note> of any other, since all things are in Him, and He alone is in all things. This is true unless anyone dares to say that His motion consists of eternity; but rather, His eternity is immovable, into which

# The COMMENTARY.

*The eleventh Chapter reveals that only God is immovable and constant, and all other things are subject to change <note>original: "mutable"</note>; God is infinite, beyond human understanding <note>original: "incomprehensible"</note>, and his true nature is unknown. This chapter discusses the holy, pure, and eternal perception of God, the perception of the world, the perception of man, and the difference between intellectual understanding and physical sense. This is the summary of the eleventh part.*

### Hermes Trismegistus.

...so many different shapes, just as the course of the world has distinct moments. Thus, the Forms of each kind are permanent and stable, yet individuals are unlike one another within that same Form.

*Asclepius:* "And does the world change its **Species**, or Form, O **Trismegistus**?"

*Trismegistus:* "Do you realize then, O *Asclepius*, that all these things are spoken to you as if you were in a sleep or a dream? For what is the world, or what does it consist of, other than all things that have been created?"

*Asclepius:* "Therefore, do you mean to speak of the Heavens, the Earth, and the Elements, **Trismegistus**? Within the Form of the Heavens, these things exist; these Forms are altered by the [Universal] Form. Likewise, the Earth always has many alterations of her Form, such as when she brings forth fruits and nourishes them, and when she provides various and diverse qualities and quantities of all fruits, places, and times—accounting for the qualities, scents <note>original: sents</note>, flavors <note>original: relishes</note>, and forms of all trees, flowers, and berries. 

The fire also has many divine alterations; as for the Forms of the Sun and the Moon, they take on all shapes, for they are (as it were) our mirrors <note>original: looking-glasses</note> for representation <note>referring to how celestial bodies reflect divine patterns or provide images for human understanding</note>. But enough of these things.

<summary>Hermes explains that the physical elements and celestial bodies are in a state of constant flux, changing their outward "Forms" to produce the variety of life and seasonal shifts observed on Earth.</summary>

This twelfth chapter declares that there is no vacuity <note>original: Vacuity; meaning a complete void or emptiness</note> or emptiness in things; that angels converse and dwell with us, who, because of their extreme subtlety <note>original: subtilty; referring here to a lack of physical density or being made of very "fine" matter</note>, are not perceived by us. ## CHAPTER XIV.

**Asclepius.** In what part of the world, therefore, do the Fates <note>The personifications of destiny who were believed to control the "thread" of human life</note> reside, O **Trismegistus**? If the Heavenly Gods hold universal rule, do the earthly gods—whom we call **Fortune** and **Fate**—live in specific, particular places?

**Trismegistus.** O **Asclepius**, it is a necessity for all things that are governed that they are always joined and linked together. This is, therefore, the efficient cause <note>term: efficient cause; the primary source of change or movement; the agent that brings something into being</note> of things: either the most high **God**, or that which is next to **God** (which second effect is also called **God** <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the "second god" often refers to the *Logos* or the physical Cosmos itself, viewed as a divine, living being</note>), or the discipline of all earthly and **heavenly** things, established by divine laws.

<summary>Asclepius asks Trismegistus where the forces of Fate and Fortune reside in relation to the heavenly Gods. Trismegistus explains that all things in the universe are linked by necessity and governed by divine laws stemming from God or a "second" divine effect.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, Fate, Fortune, Divine Law, Necessity, Efficient Cause, Cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of divine governance, specifically explaining how Fate, Necessity, and Order function together under God's will. It follows the definition of the "efficient cause" from the previous page.</meta>

<header>The Second Book of Hermes Trismegistus</header>

laws. These, therefore—**Fortune** and **Necessity**—are both tied together by an inseparable <note>original: "individual"; in this context, it means indivisible or incapable of being separated</note> knot. The former of these, **Fortune** or **Fate**, begets the beginning of all things, but **Necessity** compels <note>original: "enforceth"</note> them toward their effects, which depend on those beginnings. **Order** follows these; it is the continuity and the arrangement of time in bringing things to pass, for nothing exists without the agreement of **Order**.

In all these things, this world is perfect, for the world itself is carried by **Order**, or consists wholly of **Order**. Therefore, these three—**Fate**, **Necessity**, and **Order**—are fulfilled primarily by the will of **God**, who governs the world by His own law and divine reason. Through these, therefore, both what we will and what we refuse <note>original: "wills and nill"; to "nill" is to be unwilling or to reject</note> are fully directed <note>original: "crossed"; here meaning governed, guided, or intersected by divine providence</note> by a divine hand; for they are neither moved by any—

### The
## COMMENTARY.

*This fifteenth chapter concludes the dialogue in a religious manner with an offering of thanks. When the thanksgiving and adoration are finished, Hermes <note>referred to here as Mercurius</note>, Asclepius, Amnon, and Tatius—the four men who occupied the holy place or cloister according to the religious rites of the Egyptians—go to a pure banquet that does not include meat. This concludes the teachings of Hermes regarding the will of God.*

## THE END. <note>original: "FINIS"</note>

<summary>This final page concludes the prayer of Hermes Trismegistus with a petition for continued divine knowledge and spiritual constancy. It ends with a commentary describing the ritual meatless "pure banquet" shared by Hermes and his companions according to Egyptian custom.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mercurius, prayer, thanksgiving, vegetarianism, Egyptian rites, Asclepius, Amnon, Tatius, pure banquet, spiritual knowledge</keywords>
